 1985: 
 1986: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 1987: # Public: sending software fingerprint
 1988: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 1989: 
 1990: =head2 sending_software()
 1991: 
 1992: =head3 Purpose
 1993: 
 1994: Returns information extracted from headers that identify the software or
 1995: server-side infrastructure used to compose or inject the message.  These
 1996: headers are injected by email clients, bulk-mailing libraries, and shared
 1997: hosting control panels, and are often the most direct evidence of how the
 1998: spam was sent and from which server.
 1999: 
 2000: The C<X-PHP-Originating-Script>, C<X-Source>, and C<X-Source-Host> headers
 2001: in particular are injected automatically by many shared hosting providers
 2002: (cPanel, Plesk, DirectAdmin) and reveal the exact PHP script path and
 2003: hostname responsible.  A hosting abuse team can use these values to
 2004: identify the compromised or malicious account immediately, without needing
 2005: to search logs.
 2006: 
 2007: The data is extracted synchronously during C<parse_email()> with no network
 2008: I/O.  This method simply returns the pre-built list.
 2009: 
 2010: =head3 Usage
 2011: 
 2012:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 2013:     my @sw = $analyser->sending_software();
 2014: 
 2015:     for my $s (@sw) {
 2016:         printf "%-30s : %s\n", $s->{header}, $s->{value};
 2017:         printf "  Note: %s\n", $s->{note};
 2018:     }
 2019: 
 2020:     # Check for shared-hosting injection headers
 2021:     my @hosting = grep {
 2022:         $_->{header} =~ /^x-(?:php-originating-script|source)/
 2023:     } @sw;
 2024: 
 2025:     if (@hosting) {
 2026:         print "Shared-hosting script detected -- report to hosting abuse team:\n";
 2027:         print "  $_->{header}: $_->{value}\n" for @hosting;
 2028:     }
 2029: 
 2030:     # Extract the mailer name if present
 2031:     my ($mailer) = grep { $_->{header} eq 'x-mailer' } @sw;
 2032:     printf "Sent with: %s\n", $mailer->{value} if $mailer;
 2033: 
 2034: =head3 Arguments
 2035: 
 2036: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 2037: 
 2038: =head3 Returns
 2039: 
 2040: A list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per recognised software-fingerprint
 2041: header that was present in the message, in alphabetical order of header name.
 2042: Returns an empty list if none of the watched headers are present, or if
 2043: C<parse_email()> has not been called.
 2044: 
 2045: Each hashref contains exactly three keys, all always present:
 2046: 
 2047: =over 4
 2048: 
 2049: =item C<header> (string)
 2050: 
 2051: The header name, lower-cased.  One of the six values listed in the
 2052: Algorithm section below.
 2053: 
 2054: =item C<value> (string)
 2055: 
 2056: The header value exactly as it appeared in the message (not decoded or
 2057: transformed in any way).
 2058: 
 2059: =item C<note> (string)
 2060: 
 2061: A fixed, human-readable annotation describing what this header represents
 2062: and the recommended action.  The note string is determined by the header
 2063: name and is the same for all messages; it is not derived from the value.
 2064: See the Algorithm section for the note associated with each header.
 2065: 
 2066: =back
 2067: 
 2068: =head3 Side Effects
 2069: 
 2070: None.  All data is collected during C<parse_email()> and this method
 2071: only returns the pre-collected list.  No network I/O is performed.
 2072: 
 2073: =head3 Algorithm: headers examined
 2074: 
 2075: The following six headers are examined during C<parse_email()>.  They are
 2076: checked in alphabetical order; the result list preserves that order
 2077: (i.e. C<user-agent> appears before C<x-mailer> which appears before
 2078: C<x-php-originating-script>, etc.).  At most one entry per header name is
 2079: produced even if the header appears more than once; the first occurrence is
 2080: used.
 2081: 
 2082: =over 4
 2083: 
 2084: =item C<user-agent>
 2085: 
 2086: Note: C<"Email client identifier">
 2087: 
 2088: Set by some email clients (Thunderbird, Evolution) as an alternative to
 2089: C<X-Mailer>.  Identifies the application that composed the message.
 2090: 
 2091: =item C<x-mailer>
 2092: 
 2093: Note: C<"Email client or bulk-mailer identifier">
 2094: 
 2095: The most widely used header for identifying the sending application.
 2096: Values range from standard clients (C<"Apple Mail">, C<"Microsoft Outlook">)
 2097: to bulk-mailing libraries (C<"PHPMailer 6.0">, C<"MailMate">).  Its presence
 2098: in spam often reveals the library used to generate the campaign.
 2099: 
 2100: =item C<x-php-originating-script>
 2101: 
 2102: Note: C<"PHP script on shared hosting -- report to hosting abuse team">
 2103: 
 2104: Injected by cPanel and similar shared-hosting control panels when a PHP
 2105: script sends mail via the local MTA.  The value typically takes the form
 2106: C<uid:script.php> (e.g. C<"1000:newsletter.php">), directly identifying
 2107: the Unix user account and the script responsible.  This is the single most
 2108: actionable header for shared-hosting abuse reports.
 2109: 
 2110: =item C<x-source>
 2111: 
 2112: Note: C<"Source file on shared hosting -- report to hosting abuse team">
 2113: 
 2114: Also injected by shared-hosting platforms, typically containing the full
 2115: filesystem path to the sending script (e.g.
 2116: C<"/home/user/public_html/contact.php">).  Complements
 2117: C<X-PHP-Originating-Script>.
 2118: 
 2119: =item C<x-source-args>
 2120: 
 2121: Note: C<"Command-line args injected by shared hosting provider">
 2122: 
 2123: The command-line arguments of the process that sent the mail, injected by
 2124: some hosting platforms.  May reveal interpreter invocations or script
 2125: parameters useful for forensic analysis.
 2126: 
 2127: =item C<x-source-host>
 2128: 
 2129: Note: C<"Sending hostname injected by shared hosting provider">
 2130: 
 2131: The hostname of the server that submitted the message, injected by the
 2132: hosting platform.  Useful when the IP in the C<Received:> chain is a shared
 2133: outbound relay rather than the originating server.
 2134: 
 2135: =back
 2136: 
 2137: =head3 Notes
 2138: 
 2139: =over 4
 2140: 
 2141: =item *
 2142: 
 2143: The result list is reset to empty by each call to C<parse_email()>.  If no
 2144: watched headers are present in the current message, the list is empty.
 2145: 
 2146: =item *
 2147: 
 2148: The alphabetical ordering of entries is a side effect of iterating over
 2149: the C<%sw_notes> hash in sorted key order.  It is stable across calls on
 2150: the same message.
 2151: 
 2152: =item *
 2153: 
 2154: Header names are stored lower-cased (e.g. C<'x-mailer'>, not C<'X-Mailer'>).
 2155: Header values are stored verbatim, preserving the original case and
 2156: whitespace.
 2157: 
 2158: =item *
 2159: 
 2160: The C<note> field is a fixed annotation string chosen by the module, not
 2161: text extracted from the message.  It is safe to display directly in reports
 2162: without sanitisation.
 2163: 
 2164: =item *
 2165: 
 2166: If both C<X-PHP-Originating-Script> and C<X-Source> are present (common on
 2167: cPanel systems), both are returned as separate list entries.  A caller
 2168: building a hosting abuse report should include all entries whose C<header>
 2169: begins with C<x->.
 2170: 
 2171: =back
 2172: 
 2173: =head3 API Specification
 2174: 
 2175: =head4 Input
 2176: 
 2177:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 2178:     # No arguments.
 2179:     []
 2180: 
 2181: =head4 Output
 2182: 
 2183:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 2184: 
 2185:     # A list (possibly empty) of hashrefs, in alphabetical header-name order:
 2186:     (
 2187:         {
 2188:             type => HASHREF,
 2189:             keys => {
 2190:                 header => {
 2191:                     type  => SCALAR,
 2192:                     regex => qr/^(?:user-agent|x-mailer|x-php-originating-script
 2193:                                    |x-source|x-source-args|x-source-host)$/x,
 2194:                 },
 2195:                 value => {
 2196:                     type => SCALAR,
 2197:                     # Verbatim header value; may be any non-empty string.
 2198:                 },
 2199:                 note => {
 2200:                     type  => SCALAR,
 2201:                     # Fixed annotation string; one of the six strings
 2202:                     # documented in the Algorithm section above.
 2203:                 },
 2204:             },
 2205:         },
 2206:         # ... one hashref per recognised header present, alphabetical order
 2207:     )
 2208: 
 2209:     # Empty list when none of the six watched headers are present.
 2210: 
 2211: =cut
 2212: 
 2213: sub sending_software {
 2214:     my ($self) = @_;
 2215:     return @{ $self->{_sending_sw} };
 2216: }
 2217: 
 2218: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 2219: # Public: per-hop tracking IDs from Received: chain
 2220: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 2221: 
 2222: =head2 received_trail()
 2223: 
 2224: =head3 Purpose
 2225: 
 2226: Returns the per-hop tracking data extracted from the C<Received:> header
 2227: chain: the IP address, envelope recipient address, and server-assigned
 2228: session ID for each relay that handled the message.
 2229: 
 2230: When filing an abuse report with a transit ISP or relay operator, these
 2231: are the identifiers their postmaster team needs to look up the specific
 2232: SMTP session in their mail logs.  Without the session ID or envelope
 2233: recipient, an ISP typically cannot locate a single message among billions
 2234: of log entries; with them, the lookup takes seconds.
 2235: 
 2236: The data is extracted synchronously during C<parse_email()> with no network
 2237: I/O.  This method simply returns the pre-built list.
 2238: 
 2239: =head3 Usage
 2240: 
 2241:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 2242:     my @trail = $analyser->received_trail();
 2243: 
 2244:     for my $hop (@trail) {
 2245:         printf "Hop IP : %s\n",  $hop->{ip}       // '(unknown)';
 2246:         printf "  For  : %s\n",  $hop->{for}       if defined $hop->{for};
 2247:         printf "  ID   : %s\n",  $hop->{id}        if defined $hop->{id};
 2248:         printf "  Raw  : %s\n",  $hop->{received};
 2249:         print  "\n";
 2250:     }
 2251: 
 2252:     # Build a list of session IDs to include in an abuse report
 2253:     my @ids = map  { "$_->{ip}: id $_->{id}" }
 2254:               grep { defined $_->{id} }
 2255:               @trail;
 2256: 
 2257:     # Find which ISP handled a particular relay IP
 2258:     my ($hop) = grep { ($_->{ip} // '') eq '91.198.174.5' } @trail;
 2259:     if ($hop) {
 2260:         print "Session ID at that relay: $hop->{id}\n" if defined $hop->{id};
 2261:     }
 2262: 
 2263: =head3 Arguments
 2264: 
 2265: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 2266: 
 2267: =head3 Returns
 2268: 
 2269: A list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per C<Received:> hop from which
 2270: at least one of an IP address, an envelope recipient address, or a server
 2271: session ID could be extracted, in oldest-first order (i.e. the first element
 2272: is the outermost relay, the last element is the most recent hop before your
 2273: own server).  Returns an empty list if no C<Received:> headers are present
 2274: or none yielded any extractable data, or if C<parse_email()> has not been
 2275: called.
 2276: 
 2277: Each hashref contains exactly four keys:
 2278: 
 2279: =over 4
 2280: 
 2281: =item C<received> (string, always present)
 2282: 
 2283: The complete raw value of the C<Received:> header for this hop, exactly as
 2284: it appeared in the message.  Suitable for including verbatim in an abuse
 2285: report so the receiving ISP can see the full context.
 2286: 
 2287: =item C<ip> (string or undef)
 2288: 
 2289: The IPv4 address extracted from this C<Received:> hop, or C<undef> if no
 2290: recognisable IPv4 address was found.  Uses the same four-pattern extraction
 2291: priority as C<originating_ip()>: bracketed C<[1.2.3.4]> first, then
 2292: parenthesised, then C<from hostname addr>, then any bare dotted-quad as a
 2293: last resort.  Private, reserved, and trusted IPs are B<not> filtered here;
 2294: all IPs including RFC 1918 addresses are returned as found.  (Filtering is
 2295: applied only by C<originating_ip()>.)
 2296: 
 2297: =item C<for> (string or undef)
 2298: 
 2299: The envelope recipient address extracted from the C<for> clause of the
 2300: C<Received:> header (e.g. C<for E<lt>victim@example.comE<gt>>), or C<undef>
 2301: if no such clause is present or it does not contain a fully-qualified email
 2302: address (one with both a local part and a domain containing at least one
 2303: dot).  Bare postmaster addresses, C<for multiple recipients>, and similar
 2304: non-address forms are not captured and result in C<undef>.
 2305: 
 2306: =item C<id> (string or undef)
 2307: 
 2308: The server's internal session or queue identifier from the C<id> clause
 2309: of the C<Received:> header (e.g. C<with ESMTP id ABC123XYZ>), or C<undef>
 2310: if no C<id> clause is present.  The value is a single whitespace-delimited
 2311: token of word characters and dots; longer or more structured ID formats may
 2312: be truncated at the first whitespace boundary.
 2313: 
 2314: =back
 2315: 
 2316: =head3 Side Effects
 2317: 
 2318: None.  All data is collected during C<parse_email()> and this method only
 2319: returns the pre-collected list.  No network I/O is performed.
 2320: 
 2321: =head3 Algorithm: extraction and ordering
 2322: 
 2323: During C<parse_email()>, the C<Received:> headers are walked in reverse
 2324: message order (i.e. oldest hop first, which is the same order as
 2325: C<originating_ip()>'s chain walk).  For each header:
 2326: 
 2327: =over 4
 2328: 
 2329: =item 1.
 2330: 
 2331: The IP address is extracted using the same four-pattern priority sequence
 2332: documented in C<originating_ip()>.
 2333: 
 2334: =item 2.
 2335: 
 2336: The envelope recipient is extracted with the pattern
 2337: C<\bfor\s+E<lt>?([^\s>]+@[\w.-]+\.[\w]+)E<gt>?> (case-insensitive).  The
 2338: domain portion of the address must contain at least one dot; single-label
 2339: names such as C<postmaster> are not matched.
 2340: 
 2341: =item 3.
 2342: 
 2343: The session ID is extracted with the pattern C<\bid\s+([\w.-]+)>
 2344: (case-insensitive), capturing the first word-character token following the
 2345: keyword C<id>.
 2346: 
 2347: =item 4.
 2348: 
 2349: If none of the three fields can be extracted (all are C<undef>), the hop is
 2350: silently discarded and does not appear in the result list.  This suppresses
 2351: internal or synthetic hops that carry no useful tracking information.
 2352: 
 2353: =back
 2354: 
 2355: The result list therefore contains only hops that carry at least one
 2356: actionable piece of tracking data.
 2357: 
 2358: =head3 Notes
 2359: 
 2360: =over 4
 2361: 
 2362: =item *
 2363: 
 2364: The result list is reset to empty by each call to C<parse_email()>.  It
 2365: reflects the C<Received:> headers of the current message only.
 2366: 
 2367: =item *
 2368: 
 2369: Oldest-first ordering means C<$trail[0]> is the first relay the message
 2370: passed through after leaving the sender, and C<$trail[-1]> is the last hop
 2371: before your own server.  This is the natural order for walking the chain
 2372: when composing a forwarded abuse report.
 2373: 
 2374: =item *
 2375: 
 2376: C<ip> may be C<undef> for a hop that nonetheless has a valid C<for> or
 2377: C<id> field -- for example, a C<Received:> header added by a local
 2378: delivery agent that does not record an IP.  Always test C<defined
 2379: $hop-E<gt>{ip}> before using it.
 2380: 
 2381: =item *
 2382: 
 2383: C<for> and C<id> are C<undef>, not the empty string, when absent.  C<ip>
 2384: is also C<undef>, not C<'(unknown)'> as in some other methods.  All four
 2385: fields must be tested with C<defined>, not boolean truthiness, to
 2386: distinguish between absent and empty.
 2387: 
 2388: =item *
 2389: 
 2390: C<report()> applies an additional filter when displaying this data: it only
 2391: shows hops where C<id> or C<for> is defined, suppressing hops where only
 2392: an IP was found.  C<received_trail()> itself returns all hops with any
 2393: extractable data, including IP-only hops, giving callers the full picture.
 2394: 
 2395: =item *
 2396: 
 2397: The C<received> field is the unfolded header value as stored after RFC 2822
 2398: line-folding is removed during C<parse_email()>.  Continuation whitespace
 2399: is replaced with a single space; the value will not contain embedded
 2400: newlines.
 2401: 
 2402: =back
 2403: 
 2404: =head3 API Specification
 2405: 
 2406: =head4 Input
 2407: 
 2408:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 2409:     # No arguments.
 2410:     []
 2411: 
 2412: =head4 Output
 2413: 
 2414:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 2415: 
 2416:     # A list (possibly empty) of hashrefs, oldest-hop first:
 2417:     (
 2418:         {
 2419:             type => HASHREF,
 2420:             keys => {
 2421:                 received => {
 2422:                     type => SCALAR,
 2423:                     # Complete unfolded Received: header value; always defined.
 2424:                 },
 2425:                 ip => {
 2426:                     type     => SCALAR,
 2427:                     optional => 1,  # present but may be undef
 2428:                     regex    => qr/^\d{1,3}(?:\.\d{1,3}){3}$/,
 2429:                 },
 2430:                 for => {
 2431:                     type     => SCALAR,
 2432:                     optional => 1,  # present but may be undef
 2433:                     # Fully-qualified email address: local@domain.tld
 2434:                     regex    => qr/^[^\s@]+\@[\w.-]+\.[a-zA-Z]{2,}$/,
 2435:                 },
 2436:                 id => {
 2437:                     type     => SCALAR,
 2438:                     optional => 1,  # present but may be undef
 2439:                     regex    => qr/^[\w.-]+$/,
 2440:                 },
 2441:             },
 2442:         },
 2443:         # ... one hashref per hop with at least one extractable field,
 2444:         #     in oldest-first (outermost relay first) order
 2445:     )
 2446: 
 2447:     # Empty list when no Received: headers are present or none yielded
 2448:     # any extractable data.
 2449: 
 2450: =cut
 2451: 
 2452: sub received_trail {
 2453:     my ($self) = @_;
 2454:     return @{ $self->{_rcvd_tracking} };
 2455: }
 2456:

 2482: 
 2483: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 2484: # Public: risk assessment
 2485: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 2486: 
 2487: =head2 risk_assessment()
 2488: 
 2489: =head3 Purpose
 2490: 
 2491: Evaluates the message against a set of heuristic checks and returns an
 2492: overall risk level, a weighted numeric score, and a list of every specific
 2493: red flag that contributed to the score.
 2494: 
 2495: The assessment covers five categories: originating IP characteristics, email
 2496: authentication results, C<Date:> header validity, identity and header
 2497: consistency, and URL and domain properties.  Each finding is assigned a
 2498: severity, a machine-readable flag name, and a human-readable detail string.
 2499: 
 2500: The result is computed once and cached; subsequent calls on the same object
 2501: return the same hashref without repeating any analysis.  Calling
 2502: C<risk_assessment()> also implicitly triggers C<originating_ip()>,
 2503: C<embedded_urls()>, and C<mailto_domains()> if they have not already been
 2504: called, performing all associated network I/O.
 2505: 
 2506: =head3 Usage
 2507: 
 2508:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 2509:     my $risk = $analyser->risk_assessment();
 2510: 
 2511:     printf "Risk level : %s (score: %d)\n", $risk->{level}, $risk->{score};
 2512: 
 2513:     for my $f (@{ $risk->{flags} }) {
 2514:         printf "  [%-6s] %s\n    %s\n",
 2515:             $f->{severity}, $f->{flag}, $f->{detail};
 2516:     }
 2517: 
 2518:     # Gate an automated report on HIGH level only
 2519:     if ($risk->{level} eq 'HIGH') {
 2520:         send_abuse_report($analyser->abuse_report_text());
 2521:     }
 2522: 
 2523:     # Collect only HIGH and MEDIUM flags for a summary
 2524:     my @significant = grep { $_->{severity} =~ /^(?:HIGH|MEDIUM)$/ }
 2525:                       @{ $risk->{flags} };
 2526: 
 2527:     # Check for a specific flag
 2528:     my ($flag) = grep { $_->{flag} eq 'recently_registered_domain' }
 2529:                  @{ $risk->{flags} };
 2530:     warn "Phishing domain suspected\n" if $flag;
 2531: 
 2532:     # INFO level means no actionable red flags
 2533:     if ($risk->{level} eq 'INFO') {
 2534:         print "No significant red flags detected.\n";
 2535:     }
 2536: 
 2537: =head3 Arguments
 2538: 
 2539: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 2540: 
 2541: =head3 Returns
 2542: 
 2543: A hashref with exactly three keys, all always present:
 2544: 
 2545: =over 4
 2546: 
 2547: =item C<level> (string)
 2548: 
 2549: The overall risk classification, determined by the weighted score:
 2550: 
 2551:     Score >= 9  =>  'HIGH'
 2552:     Score >= 5  =>  'MEDIUM'
 2553:     Score >= 2  =>  'LOW'
 2554:     Score <  2  =>  'INFO'
 2555: 
 2556: C<'INFO'> means either no flags were raised or only zero-weight (INFO
 2557: severity) flags were raised.  It does not mean the message is definitely
 2558: legitimate; it means no significant heuristic evidence of spam was found.
 2559: 
 2560: =item C<score> (integer)
 2561: 
 2562: The sum of the weights of all flags raised.  Weights by severity:
 2563: 
 2564:     HIGH   => 3
 2565:     MEDIUM => 2
 2566:     LOW    => 1
 2567:     INFO   => 0
 2568: 
 2569: The score is a non-negative integer.  Multiple flags of the same severity
 2570: each contribute their full weight independently; there is no cap on the
 2571: score.
 2572: 
 2573: =item C<flags> (arrayref of hashrefs)
 2574: 
 2575: A reference to a list of flag hashrefs, one per red flag raised, in the
 2576: order they were detected.  Each hashref contains exactly three keys:
 2577: 
 2578: =over 8
 2579: 
 2580: =item C<severity> (string)
 2581: 
 2582: One of C<'HIGH'>, C<'MEDIUM'>, C<'LOW'>, or C<'INFO'>.
 2583: 
 2584: =item C<flag> (string)
 2585: 
 2586: A lower-cased, underscore-separated machine-readable identifier.  See the
 2587: Algorithm section for the full list of possible flag names.
 2588: 
 2589: =item C<detail> (string)
 2590: 
 2591: A human-readable sentence describing the specific finding, including the
 2592: values from the message that triggered the flag (domain name, IP address,
 2593: header value, etc.).  Suitable for inclusion in an abuse report or log.
 2594: 
 2595: =back
 2596: 
 2597: The arrayref is empty (C<[]>) when no flags are raised.
 2598: 
 2599: =back
 2600: 
 2601: =head3 Side Effects
 2602: 
 2603: The first call triggers C<originating_ip()>, C<embedded_urls()>, and
 2604: C<mailto_domains()> if they have not already run on the current message.
 2605: Each of those methods may perform network I/O as documented in their own
 2606: entries.  Specifically:
 2607: 
 2608: =over 4
 2609: 
 2610: =item * C<originating_ip()> performs a PTR lookup and RDAP/WHOIS for the
 2611: sending IP.
 2612: 
 2613: =item * C<embedded_urls()> performs an A lookup and RDAP/WHOIS for each
 2614: unique URL hostname.
 2615: 
 2616: =item * C<mailto_domains()> performs A, MX, NS, and WHOIS queries for
 2617: each unique contact domain.
 2618: 
 2619: =back
 2620: 
 2621: All results are cached.  Subsequent calls to C<risk_assessment()> on the
 2622: same object return the cached hashref immediately.  The cache is invalidated
 2623: by C<parse_email()>.
 2624: 
 2625: =head3 Algorithm: flags and scoring
 2626: 
 2627: The following flags may be raised.  They are evaluated in five groups, in
 2628: the order shown.  The same flag name is never raised more than once per
 2629: message.
 2630: 
 2631: B<Group 1 -- Originating IP> (requires C<originating_ip()> to return a
 2632: result):
 2633: 
 2634: =over 4
 2635: 
 2636: =item C<residential_sending_ip> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2637: 
 2638: The rDNS of the sending IP matches patterns associated with residential
 2639: broadband or dynamically-assigned addresses: an embedded dotted-quad, or
 2640: any of the substrings C<dsl>, C<adsl>, C<cable>, C<broad>, C<dial>,
 2641: C<dynamic>, C<dhcp>, C<ppp>, C<residential>, C<cust>, C<home>, C<pool>,
 2642: C<client>, C<user>, C<staticN>, or C<hostN>.
 2643: 
 2644: =item C<no_reverse_dns> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2645: 
 2646: The sending IP has no PTR record, or the PTR lookup returned the sentinel
 2647: C<'(no reverse DNS)'>.  Legitimate mail servers invariably have rDNS.
 2648: 
 2649: =item C<low_confidence_origin> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2650: 
 2651: The originating IP was taken from an unverified header (C<X-Originating-IP>)
 2652: rather than from the C<Received:> chain.  Confidence level is C<'low'>.
 2653: 
 2654: =item C<high_spam_country> (INFO, weight 0)
 2655: 
 2656: The sending IP's country code is one of: C<CN> (China), C<RU> (Russia),
 2657: C<NG> (Nigeria), C<VN> (Vietnam), C<IN> (India), C<PK> (Pakistan),
 2658: C<BD> (Bangladesh).  Informational only; does not contribute to the score.
 2659: 
 2660: =back
 2661: 
 2662: B<Group 2 -- Email authentication> (from C<Authentication-Results:> header):
 2663: 
 2664: =over 4
 2665: 
 2666: =item C<spf_fail> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2667: 
 2668: SPF result is C<fail>, C<permerror>, C<temperror>, C<none>, or any value
 2669: other than C<pass> or C<softfail>.  The sending IP is not authorised by
 2670: the domain's SPF record.
 2671: 
 2672: =item C<spf_softfail> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2673: 
 2674: SPF result is C<softfail> (C<~all>).  The sending IP is not explicitly
 2675: authorised but the domain policy does not hard-fail it.
 2676: 
 2677: =item C<dkim_fail> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2678: 
 2679: DKIM result is present and is any value other than C<pass>.
 2680: 
 2681: =item C<dmarc_fail> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2682: 
 2683: DMARC result is present and is any value other than C<pass>.
 2684: 
 2685: =item C<dkim_domain_mismatch> (INFO or MEDIUM, weight 0 or 2)
 2686: 
 2687: The DKIM signing domain (C<d=> tag) differs from the registrable domain
 2688: of the C<From:> address.  Raised at INFO (weight 0) when DKIM passes --
 2689: this is normal for bulk senders using ESPs such as SendGrid or Mailchimp.
 2690: Raised at MEDIUM (weight 2) when DKIM fails or is absent -- a differing
 2691: domain combined with a failed signature is more suspicious.
 2692: 
 2693: =back
 2694: 
 2695: B<Group 3 -- Date: header>:
 2696: 
 2697: =over 4
 2698: 
 2699: =item C<missing_date> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2700: 
 2701: No C<Date:> header is present, or it contains only whitespace.  Violates
 2702: RFC 5322; common in programmatically-generated spam.
 2703: 
 2704: =item C<suspicious_date> (LOW, weight 1)
 2705: 
 2706: The C<Date:> header is present but more than 7 days in the past or more
 2707: than 7 days in the future relative to the time of analysis.  Timezone
 2708: offsets are ignored during comparison (maximum error: approximately 14
 2709: hours, well within the 7-day window).
 2710: 
 2711: =item C<implausible_timezone> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2712: 
 2713: The C<Date:> header contains a numeric timezone offset outside the range
 2714: of all real and historical timezones: positive offsets beyond C<+1400>
 2715: (Line Islands, UTC+14) or negative offsets beyond C<-1200> (Baker Island,
 2716: UTC-12), or a minutes field of 60 or more.  Such values cannot appear in
 2717: legitimate mail and are a reliable indicator of a machine-generated or
 2718: forged header.
 2719: 
 2720: =back
 2721: 
 2722: B<Group 4 -- Header identity and consistency>:
 2723: 
 2724: =over 4
 2725: 
 2726: =item C<display_name_domain_spoof> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2727: 
 2728: The C<From:> display name contains a domain name (matched against the
 2729: suffixes C<.com>, C<.net>, C<.org>, C<.io>, C<.co>, C<.uk>, C<.au>,
 2730: C<.gov>, C<.edu>) that differs at the registrable level from the actual
 2731: C<From:> address domain.  Example: C<"PayPal paypal.com" E<lt>phish@evil.exampleE<gt>>.
 2732: 
 2733: =item C<free_webmail_sender> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2734: 
 2735: The C<From:> address belongs to a free webmail provider: Gmail, Yahoo,
 2736: Hotmail, Outlook, Live, AOL, ProtonMail, Yandex, or mail.ru.
 2737: 
 2738: =item C<reply_to_differs_from_from> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2739: 
 2740: A C<Reply-To:> header is present and its email address differs from the
 2741: C<From:> address (case-insensitive comparison).  Replies will be harvested
 2742: by a different address than the apparent sender.
 2743: 
 2744: =item C<undisclosed_recipients> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2745: 
 2746: The C<To:> header is absent, empty, contains the string C<undisclosed>, or
 2747: matches the group-syntax sentinel C<:;>.
 2748: 
 2749: =item C<encoded_subject> (LOW, weight 1)
 2750: 
 2751: The C<Subject:> header contains a MIME encoded-word sequence
 2752: (C<=?charset?encoding?text?=>).  Often used to evade keyword filters.
 2753: 
 2754: =back
 2755: 
 2756: B<Group 5 -- URLs and domains> (from C<embedded_urls()> and
 2757: C<mailto_domains()>):
 2758: 
 2759: =over 4
 2760: 
 2761: =item C<url_shortener> (MEDIUM, weight 2)
 2762: 
 2763: At least one URL hostname is in the built-in URL shortener list (over 25
 2764: services including C<bit.ly>, C<tinyurl.com>, C<t.co>, C<ow.ly>, etc.).
 2765: Raised at most once per unique shortener hostname per message.
 2766: 
 2767: =item C<http_not_https> (LOW, weight 1)
 2768: 
 2769: At least one URL uses the plain C<http://> scheme rather than C<https://>.
 2770: Raised at most once per unique hostname.
 2771: 
 2772: =item C<recently_registered_domain> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2773: 
 2774: At least one contact domain was registered less than 180 days before the
 2775: time of analysis.
 2776: 
 2777: =item C<domain_expires_soon> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2778: 
 2779: At least one contact domain expires within the next 30 days.  Suggests a
 2780: throwaway domain.
 2781: 
 2782: =item C<domain_expired> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2783: 
 2784: At least one contact domain has already passed its expiry date.
 2785: 
 2786: =item C<lookalike_domain> (HIGH, weight 3)
 2787: 
 2788: At least one contact domain contains the name of a well-known brand
 2789: (C<paypal>, C<apple>, C<google>, C<amazon>, C<microsoft>, C<netflix>,
 2790: C<ebay>, C<instagram>, C<facebook>, C<twitter>, C<linkedin>,
 2791: C<bankofamerica>, C<wellsfargo>, C<chase>, C<barclays>, C<hsbc>,
 2792: C<lloyds>, C<santander>) but is not the brand's own canonical domain
 2793: (e.g. C<paypal.com>, C<paypal.co.uk>).
 2794: 
 2795: =back
 2796: 
 2797: =head3 Notes
 2798: 
 2799: =over 4
 2800: 
 2801: =item *
 2802: 
 2803: The C<flags> arrayref is a reference to the module's internal list.
 2804: Callers must not modify it.  To iterate safely, use C<@{ $risk-E<gt>{flags} }>.
 2805: 
 2806: =item *
 2807: 
 2808: Flags are not deduplicated across categories.  If C<spf_fail> and
 2809: C<dkim_fail> both apply, both appear in the list and both contribute to
 2810: the score.
 2811: 
 2812: =item *
 2813: 
 2814: C<high_spam_country> and C<dkim_domain_mismatch> (when DKIM passes)
 2815: contribute zero to the score.  Their presence does not change the level
 2816: classification, but they appear in the C<flags> list so callers can
 2817: include them in reports.
 2818: 
 2819: =item *
 2820: 
 2821: The level thresholds are fixed constants: HIGH >= 9, MEDIUM >= 5, LOW >= 2,
 2822: INFO < 2.  They are not configurable.
 2823: 
 2824: =item *
 2825: 
 2826: C<risk_assessment()> does not directly raise flags for domains found only
 2827: in URLs (C<embedded_urls()> hosts); domain checks in Group 5 apply only
 2828: to domains from C<mailto_domains()>.  URL hostname checks (shorteners,
 2829: HTTP) use the C<embedded_urls()> list.
 2830: 
 2831: =item *
 2832: 
 2833: If C<parse_email()> has not been called, or was called with an empty or
 2834: malformed message, C<risk_assessment()> returns a valid hashref with
 2835: C<level =E<gt> 'INFO'>, C<score =E<gt> 0>, and C<flags =E<gt> []>.
 2836: 
 2837: =back
 2838: 
 2839: =head3 API Specification
 2840: 
 2841: =head4 Input
 2842: 
 2843:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 2844:     # No arguments.
 2845:     []
 2846: 
 2847: =head4 Output
 2848: 
 2849:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 2850:     {
 2851:         type => HASHREF,
 2852:         keys => {
 2853:             level => {
 2854:                 type  => SCALAR,
 2855:                 regex => qr/^(?:HIGH|MEDIUM|LOW|INFO)$/,
 2856:             },
 2857:             score => {
 2858:                 type  => SCALAR,
 2859:                 regex => qr/^\d+$/,  # non-negative integer
 2860:             },
 2861:             flags => {
 2862:                 type => ARRAYREF,
 2863:                 # Reference to a list (possibly empty) of hashrefs:
 2864:                 # [
 2865:                 #   {
 2866:                 #     severity => qr/^(?:HIGH|MEDIUM|LOW|INFO)$/,
 2867:                 #     flag     => qr/^[a-z][a-z0-9_]+$/,
 2868:                 #     detail   => SCALAR,  # human-readable string
 2869:                 #   },
 2870:                 #   ...
 2871:                 # ]
 2872:             },
 2873:         },
 2874:     }
 2875: 
 2876: =cut
 2877:

 3205: 
 3206: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 3207: # Public: ready-to-send abuse report text
 3208: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 3209: 
 3210: =head2 abuse_report_text()
 3211: 
 3212: =head3 Purpose
 3213: 
 3214: Produces a compact, plain-text string intended to be sent as the body of
 3215: an abuse report email to an ISP or hosting provider.  It summarises the
 3216: risk level, lists every red flag with its detail, identifies the originating
 3217: IP and its network owner, lists the abuse contacts, and appends the complete
 3218: message headers so the recipient can trace the session in their own logs.
 3219: 
 3220: The message body is intentionally omitted to keep the report concise.
 3221: Headers are sufficient for an ISP to locate the relevant mail session; the
 3222: body adds bulk without aiding the investigation.
 3223: 
 3224: This method is the companion to C<abuse_contacts()>: call
 3225: C<abuse_contacts()> to obtain the addresses to send the report to, and
 3226: C<abuse_report_text()> to obtain the text to send.  Use C<report()> instead
 3227: when you want a comprehensive analyst-facing document rather than a
 3228: send-ready ISP report.
 3229: 
 3230: =head3 Usage
 3231: 
 3232:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 3233: 
 3234:     my $text     = $analyser->abuse_report_text();
 3235:     my @contacts = $analyser->abuse_contacts();
 3236: 
 3237:     for my $c (@contacts) {
 3238:         send_email(
 3239:             to      => $c->{address},
 3240:             subject => 'Abuse report: ' . ($analyser->originating_ip()->{ip} // 'unknown'),
 3241:             body    => $text,
 3242:         );
 3243:     }
 3244: 
 3245:     # Print to stdout for manual review before sending
 3246:     print $text;
 3247: 
 3248:     # Write to file for a ticketing system
 3249:     open my $fh, '>', 'abuse_report.txt' or die $!;
 3250:     print $fh $text;
 3251:     close $fh;
 3252: 
 3253: =head3 Arguments
 3254: 
 3255: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 3256: 
 3257: =head3 Returns
 3258: 
 3259: A plain scalar string containing the report text.  The string is
 3260: newline-terminated and uses Unix line endings (C<\n>) throughout.
 3261: The string is never empty; it always contains at least the boilerplate
 3262: introduction and the risk-level line, even if no red flags were found.
 3263: 
 3264: The report is structured as follows, in order:
 3265: 
 3266: =over 4
 3267: 
 3268: =item 1. Introduction
 3269: 
 3270: Two fixed lines:
 3271: 
 3272:     This is an automated abuse report generated by Email::Abuse::Investigator.
 3273:     Please investigate the following spam/phishing message.
 3274: 
 3275: =item 2. Risk level
 3276: 
 3277:     RISK LEVEL: HIGH (score: 11)
 3278: 
 3279: =item 3. Red flags (omitted if no flags were raised)
 3280: 
 3281:     RED FLAGS IDENTIFIED:
 3282:       [HIGH] firmluminary.com was registered 2025-09-01 (less than 180 days ago)
 3283:       [MEDIUM] rDNS 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au looks like a broadband line
 3284:       ...
 3285: 
 3286: Each flag is formatted as C<[SEVERITY] detail-string>, one per line,
 3287: indented two spaces.  The flag machine-name is not included; only the
 3288: human-readable detail string is shown, matching what a postmaster would
 3289: want to read.
 3290: 
 3291: =item 4. Originating IP (omitted if C<originating_ip()> returns C<undef>)
 3292: 
 3293:     ORIGINATING IP: 120.88.161.249 (120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au)
 3294:     NETWORK OWNER:  TPG Telecom Limited
 3295: 
 3296: =item 5. Abuse contacts (omitted if C<abuse_contacts()> returns an empty list)
 3297: 
 3298:     ABUSE CONTACTS:
 3299:       abuse@tpg.com.au (Sending ISP)
 3300:       abuse@registrar.example (Domain registrar for firmluminary.com)
 3301: 
 3302: =item 6. Original message headers
 3303: 
 3304:     ------------------------------------------------------------------------
 3305:     ORIGINAL MESSAGE HEADERS:
 3306:     ------------------------------------------------------------------------
 3307:     received: from 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au ...
 3308:     from: Sender <spammer@firmluminary.com>
 3309:     ...
 3310: 
 3311: All parsed headers are emitted, one per line, in the order they appeared
 3312: in the original message.  Header names are lower-cased (as normalised
 3313: during C<parse_email()>).  Header values are verbatim.  The message
 3314: body is not included.
 3315: 
 3316: =back
 3317: 
 3318: =head3 Side Effects
 3319: 
 3320: Calls C<risk_assessment()>, C<originating_ip()>, and C<abuse_contacts()>
 3321: if they have not already run, which in turn may perform network I/O as
 3322: documented in those methods.  All results are cached; the text is not
 3323: itself cached, but re-computing it is cheap since all the underlying data
 3324: is already cached.
 3325: 
 3326: =head3 Notes
 3327: 
 3328: =over 4
 3329: 
 3330: =item *
 3331: 
 3332: Header names in the output are lower-cased (e.g. C<from:>, C<received:>),
 3333: because that is how they are stored internally after C<parse_email()>
 3334: normalises them.  Postmasters are accustomed to receiving headers in their
 3335: original mixed case; if canonical capitalisation is required, a simple
 3336: substitution (C<s/^([\w-]+)/\u\L$1/>) will restore it.
 3337: 
 3338: =item *
 3339: 
 3340: The message body is deliberately excluded.  This avoids transmitting
 3341: potentially malicious or offensive content to third parties, keeps the
 3342: report below common size limits for abuse mailboxes, and is consistent
 3343: with the RFC 2646 / ARF (Abuse Reporting Format) practice of including
 3344: only the headers in a first-contact report.  To include the body, callers
 3345: can append C<$self-E<gt>{_raw}> directly, though this is not recommended.
 3346: 
 3347: =item *
 3348: 
 3349: The separator lines are exactly 72 hyphens (C<-> x 72), matching the
 3350: separator width used by C<report()>.
 3351: 
 3352: =item *
 3353: 
 3354: The output is suitable for use as a plain-text email body.  It is not
 3355: ARF (RFC 5965) compliant; it does not include a C<message/feedback-report>
 3356: MIME part.  For ARF-compliant reporting, use the output of this method as
 3357: the human-readable first part and add the ARF metadata separately.
 3358: 
 3359: =item *
 3360: 
 3361: If C<parse_email()> has not been called, all sections that depend on
 3362: analysis will be empty (no flags, no originating IP, no contacts) and the
 3363: header section will be blank.  The method will not die.
 3364: 
 3365: =back
 3366: 
 3367: =head3 API Specification
 3368: 
 3369: =head4 Input
 3370: 
 3371:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 3372:     # No arguments.
 3373:     []
 3374: 
 3375: =head4 Output
 3376: 
 3377:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 3378:     {
 3379:         type  => SCALAR,
 3380:         # Non-empty plain-text string, newline-terminated.
 3381:         # Always defined; never undef.
 3382:         # Line endings: Unix LF (\n) only.
 3383:         # Minimum content: introduction + risk-level line.
 3384:     }
 3385: 
 3386: =cut
 3387:

 3448: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 3449: # Public: consolidated abuse contact list
 3450: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 3451: 
 3452: =head2 abuse_contacts()
 3453: 
 3454: =head3 Purpose
 3455: 
 3456: Collates the complete set of parties that should receive an abuse report
 3457: for this message: the ISP that owns the sending IP, the operators of every
 3458: URL host, the web, mail, and DNS hosts of every contact domain, each
 3459: domain's registrar, the webmail or ESP account provider identified from
 3460: key headers, the DKIM signing organisation, and the ESP identified via
 3461: the C<List-Unsubscribe:> header.
 3462: 
 3463: For each party the method produces the role description, the abuse email
 3464: address, a supporting note, and the source of the information.  Addresses
 3465: are deduplicated globally: if the same address is discovered through
 3466: multiple routes (e.g. Google as both the sending ISP and the owner of a
 3467: blogspot.com URL in the message body), it appears only once.  The C<role>
 3468: string for that entry is the combined description of all routes that found
 3469: it, joined by C<" and ">, and the C<roles> key holds the individual role
 3470: strings as an arrayref.
 3471: 
 3472: This method is designed to be used together with C<abuse_report_text()>:
 3473: iterate over the returned contacts to obtain the list of addresses, and
 3474: send the text from C<abuse_report_text()> to each one.
 3475: 
 3476: =head3 Usage
 3477: 
 3478:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 3479:     my @contacts = $analyser->abuse_contacts();
 3480: 
 3481:     for my $c (@contacts) {
 3482:         printf "Role    : %s\n", $c->{role};
 3483:         printf "Send to : %s\n", $c->{address};
 3484:         printf "Note    : %s\n", $c->{note}  if $c->{note};
 3485:         printf "Source  : %s\n", $c->{via};
 3486:         print  "\n";
 3487:     }
 3488: 
 3489:     # Collect addresses for sending
 3490:     my @addresses = map { $_->{address} } @contacts;
 3491: 
 3492:     # Filter to WHOIS-discovered contacts only
 3493:     my @whois_contacts = grep { $_->{via} =~ /whois/ } @contacts;
 3494: 
 3495:     # Check whether any registrar abuse contacts were found
 3496:     my @registrar = grep { $_->{role} =~ /registrar/ } @contacts;
 3497: 
 3498: =head3 Arguments
 3499: 
 3500: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 3501: 
 3502: =head3 Returns
 3503: 
 3504: A list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per unique abuse contact address,
 3505: in the order they were first discovered.  Returns an empty list if no
 3506: actionable abuse contacts can be determined, or if C<parse_email()> has
 3507: not been called.
 3508: 
 3509: Each hashref contains the following keys, all always present:
 3510: 
 3511: =over 4
 3512: 
 3513: =item C<role> (string)
 3514: 
 3515: A human-readable description of the party's relationship to the message.
 3516: When the same address was found via multiple discovery routes, the role
 3517: strings from each route are joined with C<" and "> (e.g.
 3518: C<"Sending ISP (provider table) and URL host (provider table)">).
 3519: See the Algorithm section for the full set of role string patterns.
 3520: 
 3521: =item C<roles> (arrayref of strings)
 3522: 
 3523: The individual role strings for each discovery route that found this
 3524: address, in discovery order.  Contains exactly one element when the
 3525: address was found via a single route; two or more elements when multiple
 3526: routes converged on the same address.  The C<role> key is always the
 3527: C<join(' and ', @{$c->{roles}})> of this arrayref.
 3528: 
 3529: =item C<address> (string)
 3530: 
 3531: The abuse contact email address, lower-cased.  Always contains an C<@>
 3532: sign.  Deduplicated globally: each distinct address appears at most once
 3533: across the entire list, regardless of how many discovery routes found it.
 3534: 
 3535: =item C<note> (string)
 3536: 
 3537: Supporting information about why this party was identified and what action
 3538: to request.  For provider-table entries this is the note from the built-in
 3539: table (which may include a URL to a web-based abuse form).  For WHOIS- and
 3540: RDAP-discovered entries this describes the IP block or domain involved.
 3541: Always defined; may be the empty string for entries where no note is
 3542: available.  When roles are merged, this reflects the note from the first
 3543: discovery route.
 3544: 
 3545: =item C<via> (string)
 3546: 
 3547: The discovery method for the first route that found this address.  One of:
 3548: 
 3549: =over 8
 3550: 
 3551: =item C<'provider-table'>
 3552: 
 3553: The address was found in the module's built-in table of well-known
 3554: providers (Google, Microsoft, Cloudflare, SendGrid, Mailchimp, etc.).
 3555: Provider-table addresses take priority over WHOIS for the same entity
 3556: because they are curated and point to the right team, whereas generic
 3557: WHOIS contacts sometimes route to NOCs rather than abuse desks.
 3558: 
 3559: =item C<'ip-whois'>
 3560: 
 3561: The address was obtained from an RDAP or WHOIS lookup on an IP block
 3562: (the sending IP, a URL host IP, or an MX/NS IP).
 3563: 
 3564: =item C<'domain-whois'>
 3565: 
 3566: The address was obtained from a WHOIS lookup on a domain name (registrar
 3567: abuse contact from the C<Registrar Abuse Contact Email:> or equivalent
 3568: field).
 3569: 
 3570: =back
 3571: 
 3572: =back
 3573: 
 3574: =head3 Side Effects
 3575: 
 3576: Triggers C<originating_ip()>, C<embedded_urls()>, and C<mailto_domains()>
 3577: if they have not already run on the current message, performing all
 3578: associated network I/O as documented in those methods.  Additionally
 3579: consults the built-in provider table and the cached authentication results;
 3580: neither requires network I/O.
 3581: 
 3582: The result is not independently cached.  Each call recomputes the contact
 3583: list from the cached results of the underlying methods.  Because those
 3584: results are cached, subsequent calls are fast (no network I/O), but they
 3585: do re-execute the collation and deduplication logic.
 3586: 
 3587: =head3 Algorithm: discovery routes
 3588: 
 3589: Contacts are discovered through six routes, applied in order.
 3590: Deduplication is global across all routes: if an address is found by
 3591: more than one route, a single entry is kept and the role strings from
 3592: every route that found it are accumulated into C<roles> and joined into
 3593: C<role>.  An entry is suppressed entirely if its address is empty, does
 3594: not contain an C<@> sign, or is the sentinel C<'(unknown)'>.
 3595: 
 3596: =over 4
 3597: 
 3598: =item Route 1 -- Sending ISP
 3599: 
 3600: The originating IP from C<originating_ip()> is looked up in the built-in
 3601: provider table (by rDNS hostname, stripping subdomains until a match is
 3602: found).  If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added with role
 3603: C<"Sending ISP (provider table)">.
 3604: 
 3605: The C<abuse> field from C<originating_ip()> (obtained from RDAP/WHOIS) is
 3606: then added as an C<ip-whois> entry with role C<"Sending ISP">, unless it
 3607: is C<'(unknown)'>.
 3608: 
 3609: =item Route 2 -- URL hosts
 3610: 
 3611: For each unique hostname in C<embedded_urls()>, the built-in provider
 3612: table is consulted (by hostname, stripping subdomains).  If found, a
 3613: C<provider-table> entry is added with role C<"URL host (provider table)">.
 3614: 
 3615: The C<abuse> field from the URL hashref is then added as an C<ip-whois>
 3616: entry with role C<"URL host">, unless it is C<'(unknown)'>.
 3617: 
 3618: Each unique hostname is processed at most once; multiple URLs on the same
 3619: host do not generate multiple contacts.
 3620: 
 3621: =item Route 3 -- Contact domain hosting and registration
 3622: 
 3623: For each domain from C<mailto_domains()>, up to four contacts may be
 3624: generated:
 3625: 
 3626: =over 8
 3627: 
 3628: =item *
 3629: 
 3630: B<Web host>: if C<web_abuse> is present, both a provider-table lookup
 3631: on the domain name and the WHOIS-derived C<web_abuse> address are tried.
 3632: Role: C<"Web host of $domain"> or C<"Web host of $domain (provider table)">.
 3633: 
 3634: =item *
 3635: 
 3636: B<Mail host (MX)>: if C<mx_abuse> is present.
 3637: Role: C<"Mail host (MX) for $domain">, via C<ip-whois>.
 3638: 
 3639: =item *
 3640: 
 3641: B<DNS host (NS)>: if C<ns_abuse> is present.
 3642: Role: C<"DNS host (NS) for $domain">, via C<ip-whois>.
 3643: 
 3644: =item *
 3645: 
 3646: B<Domain registrar>: if C<registrar_abuse> is present.
 3647: Role: C<"Domain registrar for $domain">, via C<domain-whois>.
 3648: 
 3649: =back
 3650: 
 3651: =item Route 4 -- Account provider
 3652: 
 3653: The C<From:>, C<Reply-To:>, C<Return-Path:>, and C<Sender:> header values
 3654: are inspected in that order.  The domain portion of each address is looked
 3655: up in the built-in provider table (stripping subdomains until a match).
 3656: If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added with role
 3657: C<"Account provider ($header: $value)">.  This identifies the webmail
 3658: or ESP service that hosts the sender's account.
 3659: 
 3660: =item Route 5 -- DKIM signing organisation
 3661: 
 3662: The C<d=> tag from the C<DKIM-Signature:> header is looked up in the
 3663: built-in provider table.  If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added
 3664: with role C<"DKIM signer (provider table): $domain">.  The full domain
 3665: pipeline (web/MX/NS/WHOIS) for this domain is already handled via Route 3
 3666: through C<mailto_domains()>.
 3667: 
 3668: =item Route 6 -- ESP / bulk sender (List-Unsubscribe)
 3669: 
 3670: Both C<https://> URLs and C<mailto:> addresses in the C<List-Unsubscribe:>
 3671: header are parsed for their domains.  Each unique domain is looked up in
 3672: the built-in provider table.  If found, a C<provider-table> entry is added
 3673: with role C<"ESP / bulk sender (List-Unsubscribe: $domain)">.
 3674: 
 3675: =back
 3676: 
 3677: =head3 Notes
 3678: 
 3679: =over 4
 3680: 
 3681: =item *
 3682: 
 3683: Deduplication is by lower-cased address only.  Two contacts with different
 3684: roles but the same address result in a single entry using the data from
 3685: whichever route found it first.  The later route's role and note are
 3686: silently discarded.
 3687: 
 3688: =item *
 3689: 
 3690: The provider table contains curated entries for approximately 50
 3691: well-known domains including major webmail providers (Gmail, Outlook,
 3692: Yahoo, Apple), CDNs and hosters (Cloudflare, Fastly, Akamai, AWS,
 3693: DigitalOcean, Vultr, Hetzner, Contabo, Leaseweb, M247, OVH, Linode),
 3694: ESPs (SendGrid, Mailchimp, Mailgun, Postmark, Brevo, Klaviyo, Campaign
 3695: Monitor, Constant Contact, HubSpot), registrars (GoDaddy, Namecheap),
 3696: and ISPs (TPG, Internode).  Subdomain matching strips labels left-to-right
 3697: until a match is found, so C<mail.sendgrid.net> matches C<sendgrid.net>.
 3698: 
 3699: =item *
 3700: 
 3701: Provider-table entries take priority in the sense that they are added
 3702: first; if the WHOIS address happens to match the provider-table address,
 3703: the WHOIS entry is suppressed by deduplication.  If they differ (unusual
 3704: but possible), both are added.
 3705: 
 3706: =item *
 3707: 
 3708: The result is not cached.  If you call C<abuse_contacts()> multiple times
 3709: on the same object, the full collation runs each time.  If this is a
 3710: concern, store the result in a variable:
 3711: C<my @contacts = $analyser-E<gt>abuse_contacts()>.
 3712: 
 3713: =item *
 3714: 
 3715: An empty list is returned if the message has no usable originating IP, no
 3716: extractable URLs, no contact domains, and no recognised provider-table
 3717: matches.  This is unusual in practice but can occur for very sparse or
 3718: malformed messages.
 3719: 
 3720: =back
 3721: 
 3722: =head3 API Specification
 3723: 
 3724: =head4 Input
 3725: 
 3726:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 3727:     # No arguments.
 3728:     []
 3729: 
 3730: =head4 Output
 3731: 
 3732:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 3733: 
 3734:     # A list (possibly empty) of hashrefs, in discovery order:
 3735:     (
 3736:         {
 3737:             type => HASHREF,
 3738:             keys => {
 3739:                 role => {
 3740:                     type => SCALAR,
 3741:                     # Human-readable role description; always defined,
 3742:                     # may contain inline domain/IP/header values.
 3743:                 },
 3744:                 address => {
 3745:                     type  => SCALAR,
 3746:                     regex => qr/^[^\s@]+\@[^\s@]+$/,
 3747:                     # Lower-cased email address; unique across the list.
 3748:                 },
 3749:                 note => {
 3750:                     type => SCALAR,
 3751:                     # Supporting detail; always defined, may be empty string.
 3752:                 },
 3753:                 via => {
 3754:                     type  => SCALAR,
 3755:                     regex => qr/^(?:provider-table|ip-whois|domain-whois)$/,
 3756:                 },
 3757:             },
 3758:         },
 3759:         # ... one hashref per unique address, in first-discovered order
 3760:     )
 3761: 
 3762:     # Empty list when no actionable abuse contacts can be determined.
 3763: 
 3764: =cut
 3765:

 4084: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 4085: # Public: report
 4086: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 4087: 
 4088: =head2 report()
 4089: 
 4090: =head3 Purpose
 4091: 
 4092: Produces a comprehensive, analyst-facing plain-text report covering all
 4093: findings from every analysis method.  It is the single-document summary
 4094: of everything the module knows about a message: envelope fields, risk
 4095: assessment, originating host, sending software, received chain tracking
 4096: IDs, embedded URLs grouped by hostname, contact domain intelligence, and
 4097: the recommended abuse contacts.
 4098: 
 4099: Use C<report()> when you want a human-readable document for review,
 4100: logging, or a ticketing system.  Use C<abuse_report_text()> when you want
 4101: a compact string to transmit to an ISP abuse desk.
 4102: 
 4103: =head3 Usage
 4104: 
 4105:     $analyser->parse_email($raw);
 4106:     my $text = $analyser->report();
 4107:     print $text;
 4108: 
 4109:     # Write to a file
 4110:     open my $fh, '>', 'report.txt' or die $!;
 4111:     print $fh $analyser->report();
 4112:     close $fh;
 4113: 
 4114:     # Log the risk level line from the report
 4115:     my ($level_line) = $analyser->report() =~ /(\[ RISK ASSESSMENT: [^\]]+\])/;
 4116:     $logger->info($level_line);
 4117: 
 4118:     # Check idempotency -- safe to call multiple times
 4119:     my $r1 = $analyser->report();
 4120:     my $r2 = $analyser->report();
 4121:     # $r1 eq $r2 is always true for the same parsed message
 4122: 
 4123: =head3 Arguments
 4124: 
 4125: None.  C<parse_email()> must have been called first.
 4126: 
 4127: =head3 Returns
 4128: 
 4129: A plain scalar string containing the full report, newline-terminated,
 4130: using Unix line endings (C<\n>) throughout.  The string is never empty;
 4131: it always contains at least the header banner and envelope summary section.
 4132: 
 4133: The report is structured as nine sections separated by blank lines, in
 4134: this fixed order:
 4135: 
 4136: =over 4
 4137: 
 4138: =item 1. Banner
 4139: 
 4140:     ========================================================================
 4141:       Email::Abuse::Investigator Report  (vX.XX)
 4142:     ========================================================================
 4143: 
 4144: A row of 72 equals signs, the module name and version number, and a
 4145: closing row of 72 equals signs.
 4146: 
 4147: =item 2. Envelope summary
 4148: 
 4149: Up to six header fields, each decoded from MIME encoded-words where
 4150: applicable.  If a field was encoded, the decoded form is shown first
 4151: followed by the raw encoded original in brackets:
 4152: 
 4153:     From           : PayPal Security <phish@evil.example>
 4154:     Reply-to       : Replies <harvest@collector.example>
 4155:     Return-path    : <phish@evil.example>
 4156:     Subject        : Account Alert  [encoded: =?UTF-8?B?QWNjb3VudA==?=]
 4157:     Date           : Mon, 01 Jan 2024 00:00:00 +0000
 4158:     Message-id     : <msg001@evil.example>
 4159: 
 4160: Fields examined (in order): C<From:>, C<Reply-To:>, C<Return-Path:>,
 4161: C<Subject:>, C<Date:>, C<Message-ID:>.  Fields not present in the message
 4162: are silently omitted.
 4163: 
 4164: =item 3. Risk assessment
 4165: 
 4166:     [ RISK ASSESSMENT: HIGH (score: 11) ]
 4167:       [HIGH] firmluminary.com was registered 2025-09-01 (less than 180 days ago)
 4168:       [MEDIUM] rDNS 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au looks like a broadband/residential line
 4169:       ...
 4170: 
 4171: Or, when no flags were raised:
 4172: 
 4173:     [ RISK ASSESSMENT: INFO (score: 0) ]
 4174:       (no specific red flags detected)
 4175: 
 4176: Each flag is shown as C<[SEVERITY] detail-string>.
 4177: 
 4178: =item 4. Originating host
 4179: 
 4180:     [ ORIGINATING HOST ]
 4181:       IP           : 120.88.161.249
 4182:       Reverse DNS  : 120-88-161-249.tpgi.com.au
 4183:       Country      : AU
 4184:       Organisation : TPG Telecom Limited
 4185:       Abuse addr   : abuse@tpg.com.au
 4186:       Confidence   : high
 4187:       Note         : First external hop in Received: chain
 4188: 
 4189: Or C<(could not determine originating IP)> if C<originating_ip()> returns
 4190: C<undef>.  Fields with no value are omitted.
 4191: 
 4192: =item 5. Sending software (omitted entirely if no software headers found)
 4193: 
 4194:     [ SENDING SOFTWARE / INFRASTRUCTURE CLUES ]
 4195:       x-php-originating-script : 1000:mailer.php
 4196:       Note           : PHP script on shared hosting -- report to hosting abuse team
 4197: 
 4198: One block per detected header, with its note.
 4199: 
 4200: =item 6. Received chain tracking IDs (omitted if no hops have id or for fields)
 4201: 
 4202:     [ RECEIVED CHAIN TRACKING IDs ]
 4203:       (Supply these to the relevant ISP abuse team to trace the session)
 4204: 
 4205:       IP           : 91.198.174.5
 4206:       Envelope for : victim@bandsman.co.uk
 4207:       Server ID    : ABC123XYZ
 4208: 
 4209: Only hops that have at least one of a session ID (C<id>) or envelope
 4210: recipient (C<for>) are shown; IP-only hops are suppressed.  Oldest hop
 4211: first.
 4212: 
 4213: =item 7. Embedded HTTP/HTTPS URLs
 4214: 
 4215:     [ EMBEDDED HTTP/HTTPS URLs ]
 4216:       Host         : bit.ly  *** URL SHORTENER - real destination hidden ***
 4217:       IP           : 67.199.248.11
 4218:       Country      : US
 4219:       Organisation : Bit.ly LLC
 4220:       Abuse addr   : abuse@bit.ly
 4221:       URL          : https://bit.ly/scam123
 4222: 
 4223: URLs are grouped by hostname; if multiple URLs share a hostname, all
 4224: paths are listed together under the single host block.  Known URL
 4225: shorteners are annotated inline.  Shown as C<(none found)> when the
 4226: body contains no HTTP/HTTPS URLs.
 4227: 
 4228: =item 8. Contact / reply-to domains
 4229: 
 4230:     [ CONTACT / REPLY-TO DOMAINS ]
 4231:       Domain       : firmluminary.com
 4232:       Found in     : From: header
 4233:       *** WARNING: RECENTLY REGISTERED - possible phishing domain ***
 4234:       Registered   : 2025-09-01
 4235:       Expires      : 2026-09-01
 4236:       Registrar    : GoDaddy.com LLC
 4237:       Reg. abuse   : abuse@godaddy.com
 4238:       Web host IP  : 104.21.30.10
 4239:       Web host org : Cloudflare Inc
 4240:       Web abuse    : abuse@cloudflare.com
 4241:       MX host      : mail.firmluminary.com
 4242:       MX IP        : 198.51.100.5
 4243:       MX org       : Hosting Corp
 4244:       MX abuse     : abuse@hostingcorp.example
 4245:       NS host      : ns1.cloudflare.com
 4246:       NS IP        : 173.245.58.51
 4247:       NS org       : Cloudflare Inc
 4248:       NS abuse     : abuse@cloudflare.com
 4249: 
 4250: One block per domain from C<mailto_domains()>.  Recently-registered
 4251: domains receive an inline warning banner.  Shown as C<(none found)>
 4252: when no qualifying contact domains are present.
 4253: 
 4254: =item 9. Where to send abuse reports
 4255: 
 4256:     [ WHERE TO SEND ABUSE REPORTS ]
 4257:       Role         : Sending ISP
 4258:       Send to      : abuse@tpg.com.au
 4259:       Note         : Network owner of originating IP 120.88.161.249 (TPG Telecom)
 4260:       Discovered   : ip-whois
 4261: 
 4262:       Role         : Domain registrar for firmluminary.com
 4263:       Send to      : abuse@godaddy.com
 4264:       Note         : Registrar: GoDaddy.com LLC
 4265:       Discovered   : domain-whois
 4266: 
 4267: One block per contact from C<abuse_contacts()>.  Shown as
 4268: C<(no abuse contacts could be determined)> when the list is empty.
 4269: 
 4270: =back
 4271: 
 4272: The report ends with a closing row of 72 equals signs.
 4273: 
 4274: =head3 Side Effects
 4275: 
 4276: Calls C<risk_assessment()>, C<originating_ip()>, C<sending_software()>,
 4277: C<received_trail()>, C<embedded_urls()>, C<mailto_domains()>, and
 4278: C<abuse_contacts()> if they have not already run on the current message,
 4279: performing all associated network I/O as documented in those methods.  All
 4280: underlying results are cached; the report text itself is not cached, but
 4281: re-computation is inexpensive since the data is already available.
 4282: 
 4283: =head3 Notes
 4284: 
 4285: =over 4
 4286: 
 4287: =item *
 4288: 
 4289: The report is idempotent: calling C<report()> multiple times on the same
 4290: object always returns an identical string, because all underlying methods
 4291: are cached.
 4292: 
 4293: =item *
 4294: 
 4295: MIME encoded-words in the C<From:>, C<Subject:>, and other displayed
 4296: headers are decoded for readability.  When a header was encoded, both the
 4297: decoded form and the raw encoded original are shown, so the report is
 4298: useful both for human reading and for log parsing.
 4299: 
 4300: =item *
 4301: 
 4302: URL hosts in section 7 are grouped by hostname and shown in first-seen
 4303: order.  Multiple URLs on the same host are listed together rather than
 4304: repeating the host's IP and WHOIS information, keeping the output compact
 4305: even when a message contains dozens of tracking-pixel and click-redirect
 4306: URLs all on the same CDN.
 4307: 
 4308: =item *
 4309: 
 4310: The received-trail section (section 6) filters out hops that have only an
 4311: IP address and no C<id> or C<for> clause.  The full unfiltered trail is
 4312: available via C<received_trail()>.
 4313: 
 4314: =item *
 4315: 
 4316: Section 5 (sending software) and section 6 (received chain tracking IDs)
 4317: are entirely omitted -- no heading, no placeholder text -- when no relevant
 4318: headers are present.  All other sections always appear, using a
 4319: C<(none found)> or equivalent placeholder when their data is empty.
 4320: 
 4321: =item *
 4322: 
 4323: The version number in the banner is the value of C<$Email::Abuse::Investigator::VERSION>
 4324: at the time C<report()> is called.
 4325: 
 4326: =back
 4327: 
 4328: =head3 API Specification
 4329: 
 4330: =head4 Input
 4331: 
 4332:     # Params::Validate::Strict compatible specification
 4333:     # No arguments.
 4334:     []
 4335: 
 4336: =head4 Output
 4337: 
 4338:     # Return::Set compatible specification
 4339:     {
 4340:         type  => SCALAR,
 4341:         # Non-empty plain-text string, newline-terminated (\n).
 4342:         # Always defined; never undef.
 4343:         # Line endings: Unix LF (\n) only.
 4344:         # Structure: nine fixed sections in the order documented above,
 4345:         #            separated by blank lines, framed by 72-character
 4346:         #            equals-sign banners.
 4347:     }
 4348: 
 4349: =cut
 4350: 
 4351: =head2 form_contacts()
 4352: 
 4353: Returns the list of parties that require abuse reports to be submitted via
 4354: a web form rather than (or in addition to) email.  These are providers whose
 4355: C<%PROVIDER_ABUSE> entry has a C<form> key.  Each hashref contains the form
 4356: URL, instructions on what to paste, instructions on what to upload (if
 4357: applicable), the role, and the discovery note.
 4358: 
 4359: Returns a list (not an arrayref) of hashrefs, one per unique form contact,
 4360: in discovery order.  Returns an empty list if no form-only or form-plus-email
 4361: contacts are found.
 4362: 
 4363: Each hashref contains:
 4364: 
 4365: =over 4
 4366: 
 4367: =item C<form> (string) -- the URL of the web form
 4368: 
 4369: =item C<role> (string) -- human-readable role, same format as C<abuse_contacts()>
 4370: 
 4371: =item C<note> (string) -- supporting detail about the provider
 4372: 
 4373: =item C<form_paste> (string, optional) -- what text to paste into the form
 4374: 
 4375: =item C<form_upload> (string, optional) -- what file to attach to the form
 4376: 
 4377: =item C<via> (string) -- always C<'provider-table'>
 4378: 
 4379: =back
 4380: 
 4381: =cut
 4382:

 4747: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 4748: # Private: message parsing

 4751: # _split_message( $text )
 4752: #
 4753: # Entry criteria:
 4754: #   $text  -- a defined scalar containing a raw RFC 2822 email message,
 4755: #             with headers and body separated by a blank line (\n\n or
 4756: #             \r\n\r\n).  Both LF and CRLF line endings are accepted.
 4757: #             The text must already be dereferenced (parse_email() handles
 4758: #             the scalar-ref case before calling this method).
 4759: #             Called only from parse_email(); $self->{_sending_sw} and
 4760: #             $self->{_rcvd_tracking} must have been reset to [] by the
 4761: #             caller before this method is invoked.
 4762: #
 4763: # Exit status:
 4764: #   Returns undef (and does nothing further) if $header_block is undef or
 4765: #   contains only whitespace -- i.e. the message has no headers or is
 4766: #   empty.  Otherwise returns no meaningful value; all results are
 4767: #   communicated through side effects on $self.
 4768: #
 4769: # Side effects:
 4770: #   $self->{_headers}      set to an arrayref of { name => lc, value => raw }
 4771: #                          hashrefs, one per parsed header, in message order.
 4772: #                          Folded (multi-line) values are unfolded before
 4773: #                          parsing (RFC 2822 s2.2.3).  Header names are
 4774: #                          normalised to lower-case.
 4775: #   $self->{_received}     set to an arrayref of raw Received: header values
 4776: #                          (not decoded), in message order (most-recent first).
 4777: #   $self->{_body_plain}   set to the decoded plain-text body.  Appended to
 4778: #                          (not replaced) for each text/* part in multipart
 4779: #                          messages.
 4780: #   $self->{_body_html}    set to the decoded HTML body.  Appended to for
 4781: #                          each text/html part in multipart messages.
 4782: #   $self->{_sending_sw}   populated with { header, value, note } hashrefs
 4783: #                          for any of the six recognised software-fingerprint
 4784: #                          headers that are present, in alphabetical key order.
 4785: #   $self->{_rcvd_tracking} populated with { received, ip, for, id } hashrefs,
 4786: #                          one per Received: hop from which at least one of
 4787: #                          an IP, envelope recipient, or session ID could be
 4788: #                          extracted, in oldest-first order.
 4789: #
 4790: # Notes:
 4791: #   Delegates body decoding to _decode_multipart() for multipart/* content
 4792: #   types (which recursively handles each part) or to _decode_body() for
 4793: #   single-part messages.  If Content-Type is absent or unrecognised, the
 4794: #   body is treated as plain text with 7bit encoding.
 4795: #   The boundary parameter in multipart Content-Type is extracted with a
 4796: #   simple regex; missing or malformed boundaries cause the body to be
 4797: #   silently skipped.
 4798: #   Lines that do not match the header pattern /^([\w-]+)\s*:\s*(.*)/ are
 4799: #   silently discarded (covers blank lines and non-header preamble text).
 4800:

 4935: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 4936: # Private: Received-chain -> originating IP
 4937: # -----------------------------------------------------------------------
 4938: 
 4939: # _find_origin()
 4940: #
 4941: # Purpose:
 4942: #   Identifies the IP address of the machine that originally injected the
 4943: #   message into the public mail system by walking the Received: chain and
 4944: #   discarding every hop that belongs to private, reserved, or
 4945: #   caller-trusted infrastructure.  The first remaining (oldest external)
 4946: #   IP is enriched with reverse DNS, organisation, abuse contact, and
 4947: #   country information and returned as the origin hashref.
 4948: #
 4949: #   This is the back-end implementation for the public originating_ip()
 4950: #   method, which caches the result.  _find_origin() itself performs no
 4951: #   caching and must not be called directly; always call originating_ip().
 4952: #
 4953: # Entry criteria:
 4954: #   No arguments beyond $self.
 4955: #   $self->{_received}     must be populated (by _split_message()).
 4956: #   $self->{_headers}      must be populated (by _split_message()), so
 4957: #                          that _header_value('x-originating-ip') works.
 4958: #   $self->{trusted_relays} must be an arrayref (set by new(); may be []).
 4959: #
 4960: # Exit status:
 4961: #   Returns a hashref on success -- the same structure as originating_ip():
 4962: #     { ip, rdns, org, abuse, country, confidence, note }
 4963: #   Returns undef if no usable originating IP can be determined:
 4964: #     -- all Received: IPs are private, reserved, or trusted; AND
 4965: #     -- X-Originating-IP is absent, private, or unparseable.
 4966: #
 4967: # Side effects:
 4968: #   Performs network I/O via _enrich_ip():
 4969: #     - one PTR (rDNS) lookup for the chosen IP (_reverse_dns)
 4970: #     - one RDAP or WHOIS query for the chosen IP (_whois_ip)
 4971: #   No state is written to $self; the result is returned, not cached here.
 4972: #
 4973: # Notes:
 4974: #   The Received: chain is walked in reverse message order -- i.e. from
 4975: #   the oldest header (bottom of the header block) to the most recent --
 4976: #   so that candidates are accumulated in oldest-first order.  $candidates[0]
 4977: #   is always the outermost (first) external relay.
 4978: #
 4979: #   Confidence levels are assigned as follows:
 4980: #     'high'   -- two or more distinct external IPs found in the chain.
 4981: #                 Multiple independent relay records corroborate the origin.
 4982: #     'medium' -- exactly one external IP found in the chain.
 4983: #     'low'    -- no usable Received: IP; origin taken from
 4984: #                 X-Originating-IP, which is injected by webmail providers
 4985: #                 and is not independently verifiable.  Brackets and
 4986: #                 whitespace are stripped from the header value before use.
 4987: #
 4988: #   _is_private() treats undef and the empty string as private, so a
 4989: #   Received: header from which no IP can be extracted is silently skipped
 4990: #   without special-casing.
 4991: #
 4992: #   IPv6 addresses are not extracted by _extract_ip_from_received(); only
 4993: #   dotted-quad IPv4 addresses are considered.
 4994: 
 4995: sub _find_origin {
●4996 → 4999 → 5006●4996 → 4999 → 0 4996:     my ($self) = @_;

 5004:     }

 5278: 
 5279: # Full domain intelligence gathering
 5280: #
 5281: # _analyse_domain( $domain )
 5282: #
 5283: # Purpose:
 5284: #   Runs the complete intelligence pipeline for a single domain name:
 5285: #   resolves its web-hosting IP (A record), mail-hosting IP (MX record),
 5286: #   DNS-hosting IP (NS record), and queries WHOIS for registrar, creation
 5287: #   date, expiry date, and abuse contact.  Each IP is enriched with
 5288: #   organisation name and abuse address via RDAP or WHOIS.
 5289: #
 5290: #   The result is cached in $self->{_domain_info}{$domain} so that if
 5291: #   the same domain is encountered in multiple sources (e.g. From: header
 5292: #   and body) the network queries are only performed once.
 5293: #
 5294: #   Called exclusively from _extract_and_analyse_domains(), which merges
 5295: #   the returned hashref with the { domain, source } record to produce
 5296: #   the per-domain hashrefs returned by mailto_domains().
 5297: #
 5298: # Entry criteria:
 5299: #   $domain  -- a defined, non-empty, lower-cased domain string with no
 5300: #               trailing dot (normalisation is the caller's responsibility).
 5301: #               Must not be in %TRUSTED_DOMAINS (filtering is the caller's
 5302: #               responsibility).
 5303: #   $self->{timeout}  -- used for all DNS and WHOIS socket operations.
 5304: #   $self->{_domain_info}  -- hashref cache; may already contain an entry
 5305: #                             for $domain from a prior call.
 5306: #
 5307: # Exit status:
 5308: #   Always returns a hashref reference -- never undef, never dies.
 5309: #   The hashref may be empty ({}) if all lookups fail or return nothing.
 5310: #   Keys present depend entirely on what the lookups return; all are
 5311: #   optional.  Possible keys and their types:
 5312: #     web_ip              string  -- dotted-quad IPv4 of A record
 5313: #     web_org             string  -- organisation owning web_ip
 5314: #     web_abuse           string  -- abuse contact for web_ip network
 5315: #     mx_host             string  -- lowest-preference MX hostname
 5316: #     mx_ip               string  -- dotted-quad IPv4 of mx_host
 5317: #     mx_org              string  -- organisation owning mx_ip
 5318: #     mx_abuse            string  -- abuse contact for mx_ip network
 5319: #     ns_host             string  -- first NS hostname returned
 5320: #     ns_ip               string  -- dotted-quad IPv4 of ns_host
 5321: #     ns_org              string  -- organisation owning ns_ip
 5322: #     ns_abuse            string  -- abuse contact for ns_ip network
 5323: #     registrar           string  -- registrar name from WHOIS
 5324: #     registrar_abuse     string  -- registrar abuse email from WHOIS
 5325: #     registered          string  -- creation date YYYY-MM-DD
 5326: #     expires             string  -- expiry date YYYY-MM-DD
 5327: #     recently_registered integer -- 1 if registered < 180 days ago
 5328: #     whois_raw           string  -- first 2048 bytes of WHOIS response
 5329: #
 5330: # Side effects:
 5331: #   Network I/O, subject to $self->{timeout}, in this order:
 5332: #     1. One A record lookup (_resolve_host) for the domain itself.
 5333: #     2. If web_ip found: one RDAP/WHOIS query (_whois_ip) -- up to two
 5334: #        TCP connections (IANA referral + authoritative registry).
 5335: #     3. If Net::DNS available: one MX record lookup.
 5336: #        If MX found: one A lookup for the MX hostname, then one
 5337: #        RDAP/WHOIS query for its IP (up to two TCP connections).
 5338: #     4. If Net::DNS available: one NS record lookup.
 5339: #        If NS found: one A lookup for the NS hostname, then one
 5340: #        RDAP/WHOIS query for its IP (up to two TCP connections).
 5341: #     5. Two TCP WHOIS connections for the domain itself: first to
 5342: #        whois.iana.org to obtain the TLD's authoritative registry,
 5343: #        then to that registry.
 5344: #   Worst-case total: 3 A lookups + 1 MX lookup + 1 NS lookup +
 5345: #   3x RDAP/WHOIS IP queries (up to 6 TCP connections) +
 5346: #   2 domain WHOIS TCP connections = up to 17 network operations.
 5347: #   Writes result hashref to $self->{_domain_info}{$domain} (cache).
 5348: #
 5349: # Notes:
 5350: #   Result is served from $self->{_domain_info}{$domain} on all
 5351: #   subsequent calls for the same domain within one parse_email() lifetime.
 5352: #   The cache is invalidated (reset to {}) by parse_email().
 5353: #
 5354: #   MX selection: the lowest-preference MX record is used (most preferred
 5355: #   server).  Only the primary MX is analysed; backup MXs are ignored.
 5356: #
 5357: #   NS selection: the first NS record returned by the resolver is used.
 5358: #   DNS resolvers do not guarantee a consistent ordering; the choice of
 5359: #   nameserver may vary between calls on different machines or at different
 5360: #   times.
 5361: #
 5362: #   MX and NS lookups are skipped entirely when Net::DNS is not installed.
 5363: #   In that case mx_*, ns_* keys are never present in the result.
 5364: #
 5365: #   WHOIS date fields (registered, expires) have time and timezone
 5366: #   components stripped by removing everything from the first 'T' or 'Z'
 5367: #   onward; the stored value is a plain YYYY-MM-DD string.  Parsing
 5368: #   uses the first matching pattern from a priority list; any fields not
 5369: #   found in the WHOIS response are absent from the result (not undef).
 5370: #
 5371: #   whois_raw is truncated to the first 2048 bytes of the raw WHOIS
 5372: #   response.  Structured fields (registrar, dates, abuse) are parsed from
 5373: #   the full response before truncation.
 5374: #
 5375: #   recently_registered is set to integer 1 when present; it is absent
 5376: #   (not 0) when the domain is not recently registered or when no creation
 5377: #   date was found.  The threshold is 180 days before time() at the moment
 5378: #   of analysis.
 5379:
